[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.

Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. print("Level:", current_frame.level, "Step:", current_frame.step)

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
print(f"Nodes: {len(nodes)}, Adjacencies: {len(adj)}")

# Summarize nodes by color and size
from collections import defaultdict
by_color = defaultdict(list)
for n in nodes:
    by_color[n['color']].append((n['id'], len(n['pixels']), n['hash']))

for color, items in sorted(by_color.items()):
    print(f"Color {color}: {len(items)} nodes")
    for nid, size, h in items[:5]:
        print(f"  id={nid}, size={size}, hash={h}")

# Check for large background regions
large_nodes = [n for n in nodes if len(n['pixels']) > 100]
print(f"\nLarge nodes (>100 pixels): {len(large_nodes)}")
for n in large_nodes[:3]:
    print(f"  Color {n['color']}, size={len(n['pixels'])}, bbox approx via pixels min/max")
    px = n['pixels']
    rows = [p[0] for p in px]
    cols = [p[1] for p in px]
    print(f"    row range: {min(rows)}-{max(rows)}, col range: {min(cols)}-{max(cols)}")

# Look for small distinct objects (potential interactables)

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Level:", current_frame.level, "Step:", current_frame.step)
seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']

# Summarize nodes by color and size
from collections import defaultdict
by_color = defaultdict(list)
for n in nodes:
    pixels_count = len(n['pixels']) if isinstance(n['pixels'], list) else 1
    by_color[n['color']].append((n['id'], pixels_count, n['hash']))

for color, items in sorted(by_color.items()):
    print(f"Color {color}: {len(items)} nodes")
    for nid, size, h in items[:5]:
        print(f"  id={nid}, size={size}")

# Check for large background regions
large_nodes = [n for n in nodes if (len(n['pixels']) if isinstance(n['pixels'], list) else 1) > 100]
print(f"\nLarge nodes (>100 pixels): {len(large_nodes)}")
for n in large_nodes[:3]:
    px = n['pixels']
    if not isinstance(px, list):
        continue
    rows = [p[0] for p in px]
    cols = [p[1] for p in px]
    print(f"  Color {n['color']}, size={len(px)}, row range: {min(rows)}-{max(rows)}, col range: {min(cols)}-{max(cols)}")

# Look for small distinct objects (potential interactables) - colors that are not typical backgrounds
bg_colors = {'W', 'g', 'G', 'c', 'B'}
small_nodes = [n for n in nodes if (len(n['pixels']) if isinstance(n['pixels'], list) else 1) <= 20 and n['color'] not in bg_colors]
print(f"\nSmall non-background nodes (<=20 pixels): {len(small_nodes)}")
for n in small_nodes[:10]:
    px = n['pixels']
    if not isinstance(px, list):
        continue
    rows = [p[0] for p in px]
    cols = [p[1] for p in px]
    print(f"  Color {n['color']}, size={len(px)}, row={min(rows)}-{max(rows)}, col={min(cols)}-{max(cols)}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)
print("last_action_result:", last_action_result)

# Compare previous and current frame segmentation
prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation if len(history) >= 2 else None
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

if prev_seg:
    prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
    curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}
    
    print("\nNode changes:")
    all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
    for nid in sorted(all_ids):
        prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
        curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
        if prev_n and not curr_n:
            print(f"  Node {nid} (color={prev_n['color']}) REMOVED")
        elif not prev_n and curr_n:
            print(f"  Node {nid} (color={curr_n['color']}) ADDED, pixels={curr_n['pixels']}")
        elif prev_n and curr_n:
            if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
                print(f"  Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")
else:
    print("No previous frame to compare")

# Check current nodes
print("\nCurrent nodes:")
for n in curr_seg['nodes']:
    print(f"  id={n['id']}, color={n['color']}, pixels={n['pixels']}")

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

# Compare previous and current frame
prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking yellow (node 6):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={prev_n['color']}) REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={curr_n['color']}) ADDED, pixels={curr_n['pixels']}")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            print(f"  Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

# Check timer (node 0 - pink)
if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!

[TOOL CALL: python]
<tool_call>
<function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

# Compare previous and current frame
prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking black bar (node 5):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={prev_n['color']}) REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={curr_n['color']}) ADDED, pixels={curr_n['pixels']}")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            print(f"  Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

# Check timer (node 0 - pink)
if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

# Compare previous and current frame
prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking blue block (node 4):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={prev_n['color']}) REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={curr_n['color']}) ADDED, pixels={curr_n['pixels']}")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            print(f"  Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. <function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking charcoal (node 10):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={prev_n['color']}) REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} (color={curr_n['color']}) ADDED, pixels={curr_n['pixels']}")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            print(f"  Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. <parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking gray background:")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:10]:
        print(f"  {c}")
else:
    print("  No significant changes")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game.

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.

print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes:")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:10]:
        print(f"  {c}")
else:
    print("  No significant changes")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")

    print(f"White upper (node 3): {curr_nodes[3]['pixels']} pixels")
    print(f"White lower (node 8): {curr_nodes[8]['pixels']} pixels")

- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.

- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. - In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. - Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.

- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking node 9:")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:15]:
        print(f"  {c}")
else:
    print("  No significant changes")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")

# Check white regions
if 3 in curr_nodes and 8 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"White upper (node 3): {curr_nodes[3]['pixels']} pixels")
    print(f"White lower (node 8): {curr_nodes[8]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes:")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:10]:
        print(f"  {c}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")

print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking blue block (node 3):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:10]:
        print(f"  {c}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")

# Check white regions
white_nodes = [k for k, v in curr_nodes.items() if v['color'] == 'W']
print(f"\nWhite nodes: {white_nodes}")
for wn in white_nodes:
    print(f"  Node {wn}: {curr_nodes[wn]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>

Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. - In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. - After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.

<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking blue block (node 4):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}, color {prev_n['color']}->{curr_n['color']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:15]:
        print(f"  {c}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. - `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
print("Step:", current_frame.step, "Level:", current_frame.level)

prev_seg = history[-2].frame.segmentation
curr_seg = current_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes after clicking blue block (node 8):")
all_ids = set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys())
changes = []
for nid in sorted(all_ids):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    if prev_n and not curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} REMOVED")
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        changes.append(f"Node {nid} ADDED")
    elif prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['pixels'] != curr_n['pixels'] or prev_n['color'] != curr_n['color']:
            changes.append(f"Node {nid}: pixels {prev_n['pixels']}->{curr_n['pixels']}")

if changes:
    for c in changes[:10]:
        print(f"  {c}")

if 0 in curr_nodes:
    print(f"\nPink timer (node 0): {curr_nodes[0]['pixels']} pixels")

# Check white nodes
white_nodes = [k for k, v in curr_nodes.items() if v['color'] == 'W']
print(f"\nWhite nodes: {white_nodes}")
for wn in white_nodes:
    print(f"  Node {wn}: {curr_nodes[wn]['pixels']} pixels")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>
